## Part III — The Library of the Reach

The 65 books and the serial are printed in full in `story/Library of the Reach/`. What follows is the catalogue, the keepers, the canon the books add, and the thirteen rulings (compilation C).

### The catalogue

**Placement keys:**
- *shelf* is read in a library and never taken.
- *pile* is read where it lies in a house or camp.
- *shop* is bought from a vendor.
- *found* lies in the world and is picked up once.
- *quest* is found in the world and belongs to a keeper.
- *gift* is a keeper's reward.

Map ids are the game's. "Ruling" names the owner's ruling (r1 to r13, below) that touched the book, and how.

#### Histories and chronicles

| id | Title | In-world author | Date (SR) | Kind | Where | What it asserts (new facts) | Ruling |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| lib_a_hard_winter | The Fires on the Ground | Orrin Pale, sexton of Oakhaven | 413 | chronicle | pile, Oakhaven Chapel | The orchards went on a still morning at the back end of summer 412, and the reed-dust tasted of nothing. The milk soured that night and dogs howled at noon all winter. The sexton tolls the town bell for the dead; it rang flat in the first week of autumn, and he tied off the rope and has not tolled since. The ground froze two spades deep, so graves were dug by burning fires on them (three nights for an adult, two for a child). By Candlefast the Governor's own carriage-harness was boiling in the Greenhall copper. The dead are named: Wilf and Alban Wetherby (4, one grave), Essa Brindle (into the Flume at the sluice-gate), Tilda Hale (9), Mother Peascod, the miller Lambert Coker's Lettie, Osgood and baby, Jonas Garlick, Hobday's infant, the Sawtell brothers, Widow Dimmock, old Hesketh's sister, two Ackroyds (names withheld) and an unnamed Greenroot carter, making seventeen plus Hesketh's widow. Bettany brought him seven children that winter. The frost went out in one night in spring, and forty wagons came. | r13: Orrin Pale as sexton in 412 is fine |
| lib_a_wool_wall | The Wool Wall | Dorcas Ribble, carder at the Carders' mill | 417 | chronicle | quest (Ilse), the Hanger (p22_hanger) | Dorcas proposed the Hedge ("It's a stopper"). Eleven hounds sat in a row at the orchard edge on a Massday night at the turn of 417. The Cantor sent to the three nearest parishes; two Cantors came, and the third wrote "We have stopped being able to send". Tobin was taken at the Pellow farm in spring, and the Hedge went in that summer without him. The Consortium paid: thorn whips from the south, bramble crowns in wet sacking, and a young engineer in green and black who pegged the line in two days, a peg every ten paces. The Farrants drove the Downs flocks down, the Wethercombe folk came in behind them, the Bellwether shuttered and the fold was left open. Sheep were shorn in the square, the lanes and the Chapel porch; the mill book counts 9,000 fleeces. The mill engine ran day and night, then everyone carded by hand by lamp, and Nell sang the work-song all night. The build: a waist-deep thorn trench, a man's height of rammed wool either side, repeated, with felt and sacking over the top and straw in the roots, three men deep. Four hundred hands worked on it, plus the Governor's two, which blistered; he worked the north side. The last row went in at the North Gate, and the whole town shouted with no echo. Harrow keeps the key and the gate shuts at dusk. The thorn has grown a yard. The flocks are penned behind the tannery, and a Farrant counts them every night. | r1, r3, r7: text changed to "at the turn of the year", "at the Pellow farm in the spring" and "that summer" |
| lib_a_greenroot_road | What Came Up the Greenroot Road | Josiah Seddon, under-clerk at the Greenhall for 11 years | 418 | chronicle | pile, Greenhall | The paper of spring 413 was "grain against survey": wagons now and more to follow, in return for the right to measure the quarry and the ground beneath, with first refusal. The Governor said "Write that the Governor signs for bread", and his S goes through two lines. In Frostmonth 413 three surveyors in grey with brass staves walked the Flume bank by the Carders' mill and hung a humming glass bell on a stake above the sluice. The lease of Harvestmonth 414 gave the quarry, the shaft and "such resonances as lie beneath" for a cart of bread a week at a fixed price for nine years; the only vote against was "Ashgrove, no". In 415–416 came the humming fence, the pay-office in scrip, and the 120-ft mast on four legs bolted into the quarry rim, which took a summer to raise. The Factor wears black with green cuffs. In 417 the bells were taken by a young man of the Church who came with the Consortium's cart, minuted "Requisition, bronze, by order, no charge". The tankers go east daily, and a notice declared the oaks, the quarry, the Root and the glass Consortium property. The week after Tobin was lost, the Cantor's chair was carried out and the Queen's seal put up in its place. Seddon keeps his fair copy of the 413 paper in his press beside the decrees. | consistent with r1, r2 |
| lib_a_almanack_419 | Tench's Reach Almanack for the Year 419 | Cuthbert Tench, almanack-maker, printed at Stillwater; margins by Ezra Dunnock, farmer of the north field, Oakhaven | 419 | almanac | shop, Bastien Corvo, 1 sovereign | It sells for one Chime. It gives the year, the months and the feasts exactly as Vol VII does. It lists folk "old uses" of the eight days: Rootday sowing, Hearthday baking, Massday carting, Stasisday mending, Dawnday Chapel, Driftday journeys, Memoryday letters to the absent, Nullday rest. Margin sayings: "My gran would not fell a tree on a Rootday", and on Nullday "keep somebody by you and leave the lamp in". The Root Feast of 419 was kept loud inside the Hedge, the first time since the Hedge, singing the old planting song. Flume-reed First Reeds are tuned at the door by the Cantor. Hay and saplings regrow overnight, and there were no eels in Firstfruit 419. Harrow heard from a carter that more names were cut at Fortis this year. Ezra's last stand is the north-field corner by the oaks; the Church postponed the Last Sheaf for the Rite. On Contract Day the ground-rent is copied, not remade. Canopy is listed as "(Not presently kept.)". | r9: Vol VII months only |
| lib_a_orchard_craft | The Orchard Craft: A Short History of the Cantors of the Reach | Jessamy Yarrow, Cantor, of the Reedside Conservatory | 396 | history | shelf, Conservatory (Histories) | It was written at the Cantor-Master's request for the Conservatory library. Her teacher was Mother Sefton, who was 90 and had been taught by a woman of 90. The curriculum: a student enters at 8 and spends a year breathing on seedlings; then the Root note at 174 "a hand's width above the ground"; the clay-jar trick; the falling fifth; the calling-song, practised on the Conservatory roof at dusk; and the felling-song at 16. "A breath, a note, a fifth, a call, a farewell." The craft has no catechism. A palm flat on the bark gets a tree's yes or no. On court days a Cantor sits at the Governor's right hand. Pruning terms: "the long cane" and "letting down". Greenroot, three miles off, knew the weather by the orchards' chord. The five-note lullaby was "made for something very large that could not sleep". The Longest Night humming is done "in place of" something no one could name. "Every mending borrows." No Cantor has ever been paid in coin for a birth. | — (brushes the Cantors' Secret, Vol V §4) |
| lib_a_first_history | A First History of Oakhaven, for the Orchard School | Garra Thorne, teacher at the orchard school | 419 (spring) | history (schoolbook) | shelf, Conservatory | Oakhaven is the chief town, on a rise above the Flume. Greenroot is three miles east, downriver; the Watch Downs and the combe at Wethercombe are west; the Organum lies far east past the Woods. There are three founding stories. In the drovers' story a drover slept under the great oak on the green, called the **Old Haven**, which hummed. In the Cantors' story the town grew round their school. In the clerks' story the town began with the first Greenhall roll, 300 years old. Maelis was the last queen of the Reach. Seventeen died in the hard winter. The quarry, "north of the town", was leased in 414. The Hedge went in in the summer of 417, with children carrying fleece in their aprons. The school's custom is to write on slates and rub out. | r3: "autumn" changed to "summer"; r13: Garra Thorne as author is fine |
| lib_a_four_seconds | Four Seconds at the Margin | Gerda Stahl, of Tier Three, Cinderhold, as taken down on Broadsheet Row | 418 | chronicle (testimony) | shelf, Bonded Store | A runner in grey brought the order on a Massday in 417: pressure up by a third, before the Weighing, because the seal-tolerance had been "revised". It held nine days. On the tenth the fires went cold; the Cold Burn had reached the Margin, after the upper tiers since 412. Then came the F-sharp whistle. Of 907 on the day shift, 900 died and seven lived: six in the gauge-houses and one scrapper (Muty, unnamed). A gauge-house is a stone hut with a basalt wall a yard thick and a slot to read the needle. Her husband Odo, on the number-six head, shouted her name to warn her not to come out. The Consortium's widows' form had the cause printed already: "Seal failure". The Margin "said a word… enough". There is strike talk in the guild-halls. She came down the coast in spring 418, and the printer found her a hammer at a forge on the Lantern Yard. | — (the 417 date is inferred from Muty, Vol II) |
| lib_a_what_is_written | What Is Written: A Short Account of the Silent Veil, for Novices | Umber, scribe of the House, who had held the designation 51 years | 410 | history | shelf, Scriptorium (Histories) | The long path through the willows, the round arch and the pitched-hand motto. The House stands above the pool and is 1,100 years old; the Veil is older, and its earlier books were burned or drowned. The three rules are learned before letters. A scribe stands "a little way off with a board". The designation comes from the ink-cupboard, and the scribe's own name is "kept". The scriptorium has 14 sloped desks in two rows; the east stove is lit at the second bell; the desks are cleaned with vinegar. A stair below the kitchens leads to the labyrinth, and the stacks lie under the Canopy's roots; novices are not taken there. On Memoryday the duty-scribe reads a page of the dead aloud. Lanterns are set on the night of the highest water, one name each. The Veil joined the Office 110 years ago (c. 300): some wept, and three walked into the Woods. Before that the House had 312 scribes and a leaking roof, and begged vellum from the Moot; now it has wages, grey ink by the cask, and its oldest books in the Organum archive. The Registrar says "every name is a unit". In 410 the Office asked for twelve scribes for "a great register of the Woods… by name and voice and age", and Umber volunteered. | r4 (the House's own bell), r8 (designations), r12 (the hint stays) |
| lib_a_going_up | Going Up: How the Free Captains Took to the Air | Piet Ollander, keeper of the mast-tower for 22 years, Port Cantabile | 416 | history | quest (Odile), the Low Rings (flume_lowrings) | The mast-tower is 60 ft of iron rungs with a cradle at the end of the Long Mole. It once moored one ship a month and now moors four a day. A Fortis-cut core makes a hull light but cannot make it rise; drift-glass came only when the Consortium bled the Velocity country after the runic bore. The first skyships were Consortium survey craft. **Ysmay Cordell** was the first Free Captain to fly: she bought a condemned Consortium freighter at the yard auction, patched it with her mainsails, took four lift-tanks off a Synod cutter downed on the Cay, and named it the *Gull's Errand*. The Harbour ruled that anything over the harbour is "at sea, however high it goes"; the Articles board on the Mole-head derrick is chalked "and above". A tank of drift-glass costs 110 sovereigns. "The sea wants you… The air does not care." | r5: text now names both the core and the glass |

#### Lives, letters and journals

| id | Title | In-world author | Date (SR) | Kind | Where | What it asserts (new facts) | Ruling |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| lib_b_narrow_cry | A Good Narrow Cry | Damaris Teasel, midwife of Stillwater Landing for 41 years | 418 | memoir | shelf, Bonded Store | She learned from her aunt, who learned from hers. Before 412 births were loud, the men sang in the yard, and a reed from the upriver Cantors hung over the birthing door. By spring 413 every birthing room on the Flume was packed with fleece. She used a bite-strap and the words "in the Narrow, love". Keziah gave birth on a raft in the basin, and the roaring boy was hushed at the breast: "no Narrow as good as a full mouth". After the Oakhaven drill, "something came down the Flume": the still babies, which the Grafters call "infant pallor". She keeps eleven names in her book and reads them on Nullday. An upriver midwife with "a voice like a gate" (Bettany, unnamed) taught her the jar, and she hums into the crown: "a good Narrow cry". She has delivered 1,806 children. The girl who keeps the books at the Bonded Store wrote this down. | r7: hounds in the wider Reach before 417 stand |
| lib_b_stone_then_glass | Stone, Then Glass | Silas Hurlock, quarryman for 34 years | 419 | autobiography | found, the quarry | He went in at 12 under Master Cleeve. Half of Oakhaven learned to swim in the quarry's cold black water. He courted Sukey on the third ledge. A good block rings. He cut millstones as far as Greenroot, and the 11 Chapel steps at 22; the third from the top has a flaw shaped like a running hare. In the lease year (414) the quarry was pumped dry in 11 days, and it has been lit green from below since the bore. Day-hands haul and never go down the shaft. Pay is scrip, which the Boar & Dulcimer takes "at a rate". A contract crew at the pumps is never seen (Hollows, unstated). This year (419) the bore-head went, killing Marten, Old Coyle and the Tanner twins ("not twenty"); their mother was paid in scrip. He sits on the hare step on Nulldays. | — (the season of the deaths is avoided: MS ch.10 says spring, ch.1 says end of summer) |
| lib_b_the_pruner | The Pruner: A Life of Winifred Ruddock, Cantor of the Reed-Rows | Jennet Lindley, of the dye-works, her pupil at 12 | 418 | biography | shelf, Conservatory | Win Ruddock lived c. 317–391. She kept the longest row, 900 reeds from the old stone wall to the water, wore her shears on a cord, and never cut twice. She sang to apple trees, to a farrier's boy and to the Pellow cows. She tuned First Reeds by holding a raw cane to the baby's breastbone. Her sayings: "One note is a shout, two notes is a conversation"; "Never sing to a tree you would not prune". When a Church examiner came from the Organum, she sang the Root in her row and the valley answered in chords; his report was two words, "Agricultural custom" (Jennet believes the phrase began there). Win died in the late summer of 391 and was buried at the foot of her row "with her mouth free, as we did then". Her row came up out of tune the next spring. | r13 |
| lib_b_letters_from_the_gate | Letters from the Gatehouse | Cyril Tolley, junior clerk to the Consortium of Bores, to his sister Prudence ("Prue") | 418–419 | letters | pile, Depot Three (p16h_depot3) | At the coaching inn at the edge of the Reach, the landlady put her hand over his mouth. He keeps the works-gate ledger and pulls the lever for the green glass disc. The Legionnaire on the left of the gate murmurs "second shelf, behind the flour" each morning, and later, in a little girl's voice, "mind the step, Gran". The Catalogue circular is pinned inside the gatehouse door. Nails grow a quarter-inch a night. "They took all the bells in the first year of the works". A Synod man lives alone outside the Hedge and the hounds won't touch him (Kaelen). His father says engrams are templates. The Factor told him his colour was "a little anxious". The scrip will not change to sovereigns at face value. He applied for a transfer in Sheafmonth 419. The clerk is not invited to the harvest festival. | — (all weekdays check against Vol VII §6) |
| lib_b_true_and_modest | A True and Modest Account of the Life of Captain Teodor Brisk, Free Captain of the Air | Teodor Brisk, with a note by his bosun Walter Gurney | 417 | autobiography | shop, Vane, 3 | Three Long Mole taverns claim the storm he was born in. He signed the Articles at 14 and read them first. He ran the Roaring Strait eleven times by night. His ship, the *Fair Excuse*, is a 90-ft grey envelope with four lift-tanks. He has carried black glass, rum, salt mutton, cheese and a bishop, and refuses vox-bottles. He cites the Harbour-Queen's amendment to Article the First, "or until she cannot", made three years before. Gurney corrects him: the eel was a four-foot conger and the frigate a customs cutter. The Harbour-Queen printed a denial: she never dined with him and is owed eleven sovereigns, payable at the Mole office. The crew is 19 on a ship built for 9, including a deaf cook, a one-handed rigger, a near-blind lookout, two old women and a boy found crying on the Mole. "That is why she flies so low." | — |
| lib_b_year_of_nights | A Year of Nights | Godwin Pettigrew, lamplighter of Oakhaven | 418–419 | journal | shelf, Conservatory | His round is **212 hush-lamps** from the tannery steps to the top of Cooper's Row. He named the crossing by the well **Well Corner**; night-hummer Tibby Lockwood sits there and hums the lowest in town. Oil is 4 pence a pint and will be 5 by the thaw. Abigail Starling (70) vanished from the end house by the dye-works when frost split her reed; her kettle was cold and her shawl folded. He now carries six spare reeds. He heard his mother's voice at the Hedge. The Chapel's deacon complained about the second notes at Reed-Hanging. He retuned the Well Corner lamp a third above his own, making a chord. The Eel-Run was late; an eel put in your bucket is "very nearly a proposal". He dates entries by what is growing. | — (the owner notes say it shares a device with lib_c_lamplighters_note) |
| lib_b_reeds_miner | The Reeds-Miner: A Short Life of Tomas, Timber-Cutter, Against His Wishes | Gwen Fitch, his sister's daughter, for the Timber Compact's harvest supper | 419 | biography | pile, the clearing | **Reeds-miners** lifted sour wind-reed crowns (a yard down, the size of a man's head) with a long narrow spade and a lantern. Tomas's grandfather did the work, and Tomas started at 8. He was 33 in 412, with a wife. Within a month of the Fracture he had a Synod timber licence and a grey mare, **Dumpling**, who bites. He used to sing loudly. In 418 the saw took three fingers, and the fourth grew back wrong. On quota day the Synod takes the wagon and the mare if he is short. His timber shores the headframe ("I know what it holds up"). "Write that the wagons used to creak." | r13: Tomas as reeds-miner and Dumpling are fine |
| lib_b_shepherds_ear | A Shepherd's Ear: Letters from the Conservatory of Bores | Owen Hatherly, scholar of the Conservatory, to his mother at Wethercombe | 414–416 | letters | shelf, Conservatory | The Organum is "a mile-wide organ". The bellows under the Choir-Hall sound the hour; Owen stopped hearing it on the eighth day. He lodges over a cordwainer's. There are 40 first-years and he is the only one from the Reach; Kaelen's book is on every desk, unnamed. His father's two wether-bells on Hob's Knap beat against each other. Master Hollister told him he had "a shepherd's ear". Brann, from the Peaks, says you are a citizen once you stop hearing the hour. Humming a fifth is "very nearly devotional, in the wrong direction". The second-year anatomy class used a Downs ewe's larynx. Why engineers must know "where a wire would sit" is left to the fourth year. Their "lively stone" sample shifted its pitch away from the bit. Tarquin of the Organum asked whether stone could mind, and was gone by the start of term. Owen left in Lambmonth 416, saying "I am being taught… to stop hearing things". | — |
| lib_b_between_the_falls | Between the Falls | Marthe Unhaus, of Tier Eleven, for the Bellows | 418 | memoir | shelf, Bonded Store | The copy came out of the Caldera in a boot lining. Every tier has a House except the Eleventh, whose people are written "**Unhaus**" in the indenture books, and some take it as a surname. They are born owing for their mother's bed and water. The Beat has fallen in fours for 200 years except on two days, from which they count their history. People speak three counts and then stop. Her father became ember-blind after the Cold Burn ("like holding two spoons"). The Bellows are named because a bellows breathes and is never thanked. There are 400 members, who vote by tapping the pipe-rail once for aye and not at all for nay, so 400 decide in 80 falls. A three-day sit-down in spring 415 was broken with slag-folk from the Scraps. Below Tier Nine, men say the deep glass "talks". The Houses already call the coming strike the "Gasket Strike". | — |

#### Tales, verse and plays

| id | Title | In-world author | Date (SR) | Kind | Where | What it asserts (new facts) | Ruling |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| lib_c_fireside_tales | Fireside Tales of the Reach, Lately Told | Margery Fallow, who keeps the little school on Cooper's Row | 418 | folk tale | shelf, Conservatory | Within about three years the children's tales changed: the clever girl no longer sings. Three tales are given. *Clever Dorcas and the Jar*: a baby's cry is caught in a honey jar and thrown towards the Flume, hence "nobody will lend you a good jar". *Ebbo Stint, Who Answered*: his dead wife Tilda's voice speaks at the Hedge, he cries "It was forty!", and his stick is left standing by the Hedge behind the tannery; the moral is "it did not know the number until he told it". *Why the Oaks Ring*: the oaks took the ringing out of the bell-wagon and will give it back. A Consortium man says the oaks ring because the wood has gone dense since the bore drew on the Root, and that it is "not a good sign". | — |
| lib_c_tuppence_narrow | Tuppence Keeps the Narrow | Elspeth Garland (words and pictures) | 418 | children's | shelf, Conservatory | The dedication reads "For the children of Cooper's Row, who have not seen a lamb this year". A lamb is too loud, which brings the shivery air, and then too still, so she grows cold, forgets her name and goes pale. Her mother's hand's-width hum brings her back. | — |
| lib_c_still_wheel | The Still Wheel at Lathbury | Kester, a miller's man of Stillwater, as written down by a raftsman | 418 | ghost story | shelf, Bonded Store | **Lathbury Mill** stands on a leat off the Flume two miles above the lower lock; its wheel has a knock in the third paddle. The **Pargeters** (six of them) stopped sending flour in the spring after the Hedge went up. **Saul Ainsley**, the miller at the Landing, went up on a Hearthday to ask about the debt. They found a buckled empty collar, a lit hush-lamp with a dumb reed, the table laid for six with cut bread not gone hard, and footprints in the flour going to the far wall and not back. Saul is now present but his voice cannot be remembered, and he paid the Pargeters' debt himself. A pedlar on the wharf sells tales by the sheet. | — |
| lib_c_lamplighters_note | The Lamplighter's Second Note | anonymous, "a Lady of Cooper's Row" | 418 | romance | quest (Ilse), Orla's house | A Reach proposal is made at a hand's width, mouth to ear; if a third person can repeat it, it does not count. The orchard people said this kept the reeds from learning it. Mercy Hatchard (24) is the daughter of the cooper Gabe Hatchard of Cooper's Row. Crispin Whitlock is a lamplighter with a stammer; there are eleven seconds from a lamp catching to its full whistle. Mr Osgood, a works clerk, proposes in Eel-Run week. Crispin puts a two-note reed in the Hatchards' lamp, and Mercy hums the second note back. The neighbours are the Bramleys and the widow Ferrier. Gabe never reports the lamp, and is rumoured to have courted Mercy's mother with a reed. | — |
| lib_c_saffery_lightfoot | Saffery Lightfoot and the Wind That Never Stops | "Captain Teodor Brisk, of the Fair Excuse" | 418 | adventure (penny tale) | quest (Odile), the Low Rings | One penny, Broadsheet Row: "Not to be read aloud below decks". Saffery (31) sails the *Patient Jenny* with Old Mattock and the boy Fairweather, carrying 40 crates of untaxed moss-poultice to Driftwood Cay. The Consortium's poultice costs a sovereign a pot; the Reach price is a tenth of that. The Synod revenue-cutter *Rectitude* (three tanks, a brass listening-ear, Commander Grume) gives chase. **The Endless** is a wind south of Cape Dissonance that has blown west without pause since 412, and Synod regulation forbids entering it. The storm-kite *Widow's Washing* is cut loose and is still flying west. | — (in-world pulp) |
| lib_c_under_a_hands_width | Under a Hand's Width | Edwy Marchbank, reed-pruner for 31 years, now sweeps the Chapel | 418 | poetry | shelf, Conservatory | Nine poems. His master said "Listen first. Cut after." His wife Hannah and he argue about the reds (six autumns). The bells were taken "on a Massday", and nobody rang them as they went. The Hedge keeps sounds "like letters never sent". The night-hummers held the Reach together. The oaks sound "pulled too tight". His burial wishes: bind the jaw, put the stone on, leave the hands loose. A quiet child is brought round by the Cantor (418). | — |
| lib_c_bellwether_book | The Bellwether Book: Songs Writ In Behind the Bar | Reuben Thrale, landlord, and drovers; last entry by G. Thrale | c. 380–417 | songbook | pile, the Bellwether (Wethercombe) | House rule: sing a song that is not in the book and you write it in or buy a jug. Songs: *The Drove-Road* ("eight hundred ewes and a tup"); *Fold-Fire* (Long Down north, Hob's Knap east, Ewe Barrow west "on the old long mound"); *Scouring the Ram* (every seven years; scoured 395, 402 and 409, when a Farrant girl fell in); the Sheaf Song couplet ("Wethercombe cuts its own last stand to it"); *The Miller's Daughter* (not written). In a note of 413, songs over the Downs bring what this house does not name. *The Low Song* was made in the winter of 414, mostly by **Cicely** from the fold. Gideon, the nephew, took the house at Reed-Hanging 416. Nobody scoured the Ram in 416. In the back-end of 417 the Bellwether shut: the wool, the flocks, the Farrants and the fold went to Oakhaven. | see Contradictions (back-end against r3's summer) |
| lib_c_last_sheaf_play | The Last Sheaf: A Harvest Play in Three Scenes | set down by Ursula Dimmock (played Harvest Home 418) | 418 | play | shop, Bastien Corvo, 1 | The play is older than anyone. Its third part used to be *the Thief of Bread*, shouted off; since the Hedge it is *the Voice at the Gate*, which is never answered. Whoever answers brings the cider next year, and the Voice may not use a true dead name. Players: the Sheaf (a child in a straw coat), Old Reaper, the Binder, the Voice (behind a hung fleece) and the Village. Sixteen beats are hummed, with a cut on each. "I was so tired of growing back." Nobody claps, and the Sheaf-child gets the first slice of hearth bread. In 418 Hodge Lorkin answered the Voice. | — |
| lib_c_eight_keepers | The Eight Keepers, and the Sparrow Who Asked | Constance Farthing, who taught the small voices their letters | 409 | fable | gift (Moot-house keeper's reward) | Written for the charitable choir on the Cloister road, one fable a night before hymn practice; a bear was taken out. The keepers are the Badger (roots, "down"), the Salamander (hearth), the Tortoise (weight), the Heron (hours), the Cockerel (morning), the Hare (things going), the Magpie (what is remembered) and the Owl (the rests, "our nothing" against "another quiet which belongs to nobody"). The Sparrow asks each "When do you sleep?", and each answers "When somebody comes to say down for me". Nobody answers who is coming; the Sparrow asks the Badger to teach her. | **r11**: the Owl's line "We were told that somebody would" is cut |
| lib_c_hand_on_it | Hand on It: A Tale of the Eleventh, in Four Counts | Magda Rusch, of no House | 418 | folk tale | shelf, Bonded Store | A Bellows chapbook, carried west by a Deep Guild fitter. Kolb, a deaf plate-striker, has a granddaughter, Grete (11), who swears to be his ears; Ruprecht is the foreman. Kolb holds the ingot for a count of four. The whistle-sign is the flat of the hand. On a Driftday at the back end of the year the Beat missed one fall, and beat-drift froze the crew; deaf Kolb carried them behind the basalt. Five were burned and none died. Four finger-taps on the wrist now mean "he kept his hand on it". | — |

#### Craft and the natural world

| id | Title | In-world author | Date (SR) | Kind | Where | What it asserts (new facts) | Ruling |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| lib_d_yewdale_herbal | A Herbal of the Resonant Reach, with Corrections | Barnabas Yewdale, physician of Greenroot (printed Oakhaven 391); pencil notes by Prue Gorse, herb-woman, late of Greenroot | 391; notes 418–419 | herbal | shelf, Conservatory | Silver willow grows below the lower lock and in the Woods; peel it on the north side. Sweet reed-pith is now chewed raw and steadies the frightened. Resonant moss closed Jem Cutler's thigh over a nail, and the Grafters buy it by the sack. Knitbone is useless now. Lilac moon-moss from Whisper-Grove's drowned stones cures deafness; it restored a carter's boy deafened by a powder-flash on the quarry road. The lime grows by every chapel. Long drops come in a brown bottle. Mute-drops smell of bitter almond and wet slate, and the eels rose when they were poured in the Flume. There is no herb for burl: "go to whoever in your town still sings". Two cut fingers bound together heal into one. | — |
| lib_d_ears_after_dark | Ears After Dark: A Field Guide for Those Who Sit the Crossings | Tilda Dunnock, night-hummer of the Tannery Row crossing | 419 | field guide | quest (Ilse), under the Hedge (underhedge) | She has sat two years and a season since the Hedge closed, and the Watch asked her for a guide. Do not stand up. She has 22 lamps in hearing; a cradle creaks at the second and fourth hours. A hound sounds like rain starting on a slate roof and carries stolen sounds; the birds go still ahead of it like a bow-wave. A name said aloud draws faster than a scream. Hum lower. Hound beds are dry ovals where a pebble makes no sound. Never answer a voice; a trick works once. Tam (unnamed) listens to his wife. There are three kinds of silence: the Hedge's, a hound's, and the third, which "will not frighten you". A shawl folded on an empty stool. | — |
| lib_d_fellers_book | The Fellers' Book: On the Cutting of Iron-Bough | Jethro Nettleford, feller-master; postscript by Tomas | 406; postscript 419 | manual | pile, the clearing | Iron-bough is "a tree that has not yet agreed to be dead". A grove goes quiet round a stranger; an apprentice walks it for a month. Knock-notes: bronze means sound, a buzz means worm, two notes mean a split heart, a dead note means hollow. A Cantor sings a tree down first and is paid in cider; a tree felled awake took Jago Holcombe's arm. On Ringing-Wood Day the beams are named on the end-grain: two beams in the Greenhall roof carry dead fellers' names, and Jethro's bed-beam is "Not Tonight". Cut iron-bough grows a hair a year, so Reach doors stick in spring. In the postscript Jethro has been dead seven years; Tomas was his 12th and last apprentice, "for one winter". The orchard-cantor was taken in the year of the Hedge, so the trees now fall awake. Ringing-Wood Day 418 was held in the Hedge's lee and nobody came. | r9: "since Firstfruit", not "August" |
| lib_d_narrow_larder | The Narrow Larder: Receipts for a Lean Year, with Remarks | Philippa Oatley, of the cookshop by the market | 419 | cookery | shop, Bastien Corvo, 1 | Hetty asked her to write it and keeps it on her shelf. The lean years began in 412: milk turned, cheese would not set, cider went a quarter-tone flat. Hedge pottage was made without salt. Acorn cake uses iron-bough acorns leached three days in the Flume, which ring in the mortar. Eel is boiled twice and served in dock-leaf. Bread will not stop rising (one loaf came across a table). There are three cuttings of flour; Last Sheaf flour is only for hearth bread. There is no mutton, and no eggs: "the hens went quiet first, the autumn the Hedge went in". Bind a pie with honey, flour and cider. Moot-curd is the one thing that improved after 412. Quiet cooking: felted lids, no iron on iron. The hearth-bread custom: one round loaf, broken and never cut, the first piece to the eldest, the heel to the youngest and the crumbs to the hearth. | — |
| lib_d_telling_the_bees | Telling the Bees | Ennis Crowle, beekeeper of the Flume road | 418 | manual | quest (Ilse), the Flume road | Her mother and grandmother kept bees. Skeps are bound with split bramble and set on a stone stool under a straw hackle. A queenless hive howls. Since the quarry, comb is drawn overnight. Bees are told births, deaths (oldest first) and strangers, at dusk, with a two-knuckle, one-tap knock. There was a four-line telling-song, now lost. The first winter after the Hedge took the skeps' fleece, the hounds came twice and she lost two skeps. Hives are now set in the lee of the reed-banks. Honey with a drop of oil is for Cantors' torn throats, and she gives it free. | r13: Ennis as author is fine |
| lib_d_reed_and_lamp | Reed and Lamp: On Cutting Flume-Reed and Making Hush-Lamp Whistles | Dorran Sedge, keeper of the river gate | 418 | manual | shelf, Bonded Store | He was at the river gate when the orchards stopped. His theory is that Flume-reed survived because it was never taught a note. Cut it at dusk, three from a bed a day. A cut reed dies within a day unless it is "told" a pitch; a dry reed is only kindling. To make a hush-lamp: a brass collar a hand's width above the flame, the slot to the draught, a seal of beeswax (Ennis Crowle's is the best), the lamp-reed a little sharp of the Root and never tuned to a living person's note. Oil it monthly with colza. A split First Reed retuned near enough is a comfort. Reed-Hanging has been done behind shut doors these last two years. | r13 |
| lib_d_tank_and_envelope | Tank and Envelope: A Handbook for the Riggers and Tank-Tuners of Envelope Ships | Ondine Abara, yard-mistress of the drift-yard below the Long Mole; pencil notes signed "P." | 410 (4th printing 415); notes 417–419 | manual | pile, below decks on the Kestrel | Drift-glass makes a ship go. Her core is Fortis-cut lodestone in an iron cage amidships, which makes her light. A tank's "contented chord"; salt makes it moody. The tuning-hammer has a felt head and a brass shaft; tap the cradle, not the tank. "A soul is a hundredweight and a half with his boots on." A clipped tank is good for 40 miles, or 4. The 415 chapter says that below 100 ft hounds follow a sour tank. "The line is not a promise; the line is a rope." P.'s notes: weaverling silk (Deep Woods, 419) and "Eighteen. Not twenty." | **r5**: core added |
| lib_d_grafting_handbook | Handbook of the Grafting Office (Oakhaven Works Issue) | the Grafting Office; annotated by an unknown hand (Rooke is implied) | 417 | manual | pile, Rooke's surgery | Ten articles. The Grafter's duty runs to the street first, then the law, then the patient (the margin says "Read it backwards"). Inspections come every second Rootday with two sawboys, a caged brazier, an escort and a Narrow-level mask. Examination is by lens, pressure and trumpet; the dry sound means roots in the lung. Article 4, the mute-drops, quotes the canon epigraph; the drops do not relieve pain. The hush-saw and sap-iron; the bleed is green. The sap-mark means "past cutting". Article 7: the register is surrendered to any Church or Arbiterium warrant (the margin here is scraped out). Children are inspected as adults. No humming or praying aloud. Margins: "Unusual… recheck next month" and "I may go slowly". | — |
| lib_d_rudds_almanac | Rudd's Back-End Book: Signs of Weather for the Downs and the Reach | Absalom Rudd, beacon-keeper of the sheep-walk | 419 | almanac | pile, the Bellwether | He has lit the fold-fires for 40 back-ends, and half his signs have lied since 412. The first Flume-reed spear still means the frosts are done. Judge red sky by the gold now. A dog howling at noon or milk turning means nothing now. Reach winters are mild: only a skin of frost, bar the Fracture winter, and his father said the Reach is "kept warm from underneath". His office's law: light the fires when the sheaf goes up on the gate. Tamar's eleven ewes and a tup are kept to test the sheep-signs. Hearsay: beacons seen from the Organum ("rubbish"); all the oaks dropping their leaves in one night (his grandmother's legend); and a real winter "before the Reach was the Reach". | r13 |

#### Faith, law and the press

| id | Title | In-world author | Date (SR) | Kind | Where | What it asserts (new facts) | Ruling |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| lib_e_little_catechism | The Little Catechism of the Pure Note | the Choir-Church; this copy belonged to Dilly Teale of Greenroot | 416 | catechism | pile, Greenroot chapel | Issued under the seal of "His Resonance the High Patriarch", who is not named. Answers are given on one pitch. It includes the Architects and the eight Spires, "One Note. One Law. One Hand.", glass as surplus, and the holiness and sin questions. "It is a crack. Through a crack the whole jar empties." The Pure Note is 528. The official cause of the Fracture is "a dissonance was raised at the Dawning". The Hush is "a thing we do not speak of in the Church". You may not sing alone, in the open, or in two parts. The hounds won't cross the Flame. The Consecrated. Maelis "whose hand does not shake". The second note is "given to the Pure Flame". The hush-lamp prayer: "Light, keep me true". Dilly's own question: "Why has the lamp stopped whistling." | — |
| lib_e_only_song_sermon | The Only Song: A Sermon to the Flambeaux Going Out | Lucan Ostrel, **Flambeau-Preceptor** | 418 | sermon | pile, Oakhaven Chapel | Preached in the Basilica of the Pure Note at the year's "going-out" of Flambeaux, and printed so each can carry a copy. It calls the provinces "the whispering country" and their towns "six years" silent. Ostrel was hummed into the world through a clay jar. Glass-fire is "a testimony". Burning is "arithmetic": one house or the street. "The third hour of the night… is the hour the question comes." Weep afterwards, at the foot of the Spire. Be gentle with the Cantors, who are fewer every year. | — |
| lib_e_already_quiet | Already Quiet: A Word for Those Who Are Tired | an unsigned brother of the Quiet Faith | c. 419 | pamphlet | pile, the Deep Woods camp | Written to those "seven years in the Narrow". Nobody screams; "it does not hurt". The Tongueless cut their tongues so they cannot answer. Singing children are left past the last lamp, and there is "no mark on the moss". The Abbess receives letters sealed in plain grey with no device, read aloud by a brother who kept his tongue for that purpose (a hint of Vaelen). This is the 40th hand copy. | — (shelve only after the Greening, per its notes) |
| lib_e_off_the_slate | Off the Slate: Sayings of the Cantor-Master | Ilse Brennan, as copied by her pupil Hazel Thrup | 409–419 | philosophy | shelf, Conservatory | Ilse lost her voice in the throat-fever of 408 after nine nights, and wipes her slate with a wet rag. Sayings: "Do not be careful. Be exact."; "In the Chapel, one note. In the orchard, as many as the tree asks for."; the jar makes a sound "small enough to hold"; "Too loud is a good problem. Too quiet is a good problem."; "Never sing to a thing you will not stay with". On Tobin, in the spring of 417: "Make him a reason". She breathed a ring the size of a hand in the frost on a window. In 419, the slate she turned to the north field reads "CUT IT ANYWAY. QUIETLY. THINGS ARE ALLOWED TO END." | **r1**: "in the spring of 417" |
| lib_e_digest_reach_law | A Digest of the Standing Law, for Magistrates of the Resonant Reach | the Arbiterium (third issue, 414), with notes by Septimus Fallow, clerk to the bench at Oakhaven | 414; notes to Harvestmonth 419 | law | pile, the watch-house | Ten articles: (1) a writ has force from its posting at the gate; Oakhaven's writs are posted on the thorn, which keeps them. (2) Writs are read aloud before witnesses; when he did so in 413 and 414 "we lost somebody at the gate", so he now reads them into Harrow's ear and Harrow witnesses with his thumb. (3) The black-ribbon writ. (4) Attainder, with grace offered once; his letter of 416 was answered after eleven weeks. (5) Symmetry. (6) All bells, chimes and resonant metal are requisitioned with a receipt; Oakhaven's went in 417, the chapel's first. (7) No assembly of more than twelve to sing. (8) A Church warrant is not cognisable by a magistrate; only the Arbiterium may stay it. (9) Registration at the first relief station; 400 pre-numbered forms were sent. (10) A person under the sap-mark is struck from the bench roll and the census. Septimus was marked on a Massday by Rooke, and his wife "M. Fallow" signed as his sound witness. | — |
| lib_e_undertow_reach | The Undertow, Extraordinary Number: To the Reach, Which Cannot Shout | *The Undertow*, Broadsheet Row (unsigned) | 419 | pamphlet (broadsheet) | shelf, Bonded Store | A penny to the lockman, "who will charge you two". "You planted it yourselves in a night." The bell-helms murmur. Crates marked "components" go inland by air, and a loader from Driftwood Cay vanished. The Gazette called Whisper-Grove "a regrettable resonance event". A rival printer on the Row was right about the Grove. "A whole parish on the Flume road where nobody answers letters". Keep it in a hymnal at the Pure Note page. Their compositor of nine years, never a misprint, is missing. | — |
| lib_e_veil_rule | The Rule of the House: Of the Keeping of Names | the Silent Veil, copied fair by "the scribe styled Umber" | c. 290 (the Rule is far older) | law | shelf, Scriptorium | It is read on the eve of taking the pen. "We have outlived eight crowns… [and] expect to outlive the Synod, which is young." A record holds names, age, trade and the last thing said. "The world forgets on a curve… the House stands at the bottom with a cup"; "a name kept is a door left on the latch". A held name may not be spent, lent, sold or set to work; a scribe who does is "a kind of merchant". Each scribe's own name is written in the **Prior**'s hand, sealed in the scriptorium **cabinet**, and returned at the door; scribes are styled by "a colour or a creature". Memoryday is at dusk in the lower hall. The colophon: Synod surveyors came that winter and asked about the cabinet. The stamp reads "RECEIVED INTO THE OFFICE OF THE CATALOGUE… RETAINED AGAINST NEED". | r8 |
| lib_e_honest_fire | The Honest Fire: A Sermon Between the Beats | Furnace-Priest Kell | 418 | sermon | shelf, Bonded Store | Preached at the Hearth Feast forge-lighting, Stillmonth 418. The Basilica declared it "unsound"; the Deep Guilds declared the Basilica "cold" and printed it by subscription. Kell has held the Pure Note for 30 years and kept a furnace since he was 9. Heat is the only honest law. "Tell me that surplus whispers." "Everything that burns was something first." The Cold Burn is heat "behaving like a gift". | — |
| lib_e_oakhaven_prospectus | The Oakhaven Works: A Prospectus to Subscribers | the Consortium, by order of the Seven Chairs (Lorimer Stannard, Secretary to the Subscription) | Firstfruit 417 | politics | pile, Greenhall | The Second Subscription, approved under the hand of the First. The Oakhaven Root is "the most generous resonant body yet found", and it renews itself. No figures are given. It is sunk by the Vance Sequence. Pump labour is supplied under contract by the Office of the Catalogue and "asks no wage… no rest". Replacement components for the garrison come by air. A hedging scheme "of approved local design" is in hand. A dividend will be paid on the Dial Day after the casing. Shares are 10 sovereigns, in lots of 10, at the Brass Quarter counting-house or from the Resident Factor. The motto: "What the ground gives, the Consortium gathers". | — |

#### The wider world

| id | Title | In-world author | Date (SR) | Kind | Where | What it asserts (new facts) | Ruling |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| lib_f_fairweather_companion | Fairweather's Companion to the Octave, by Road, River and Sky | Jasper Fairweather, Gentleman, of No Fixed Abode (Reach-born) | 414 (4th edn) | travel | shop, Odile, 3 | The Boar & Dulcimer's hearth has not gone out in 90 years. Moot-curd "tastes faintly of thunder". At Wethercombe, the best mutton and the worst bed. At Stillwater, the lock dues are paid in advance and never refunded ("Do not ask about the board"). The Reach has murmured since the Cascade. In Sol-Luminary the upper tiers are closed; the Umbral Tiers are open, with no mirrors. Cinderhold's slag-wine. The 80-lb shield of Fortis. The Sandglass dome is two miles across. Veridia's apricots. The Salt Stair has shops on its landings. Whisper-Grove's "little traveller"; lamps on the basin; the Cloister choir. The Organum's coffee stall has stood more than half a century. Null-Watch has had "something to watch since last year". Skyships have a core and drift-glass. | **r5** |
| lib_f_overdue_ghazals | The Overdue: Five Ghazals of the Dial Floor | Nadira al-Hassur, rendered into Low Common by a copying-clerk of the Tuners' Collegium | 416 | poetry | shelf, Bonded Store | Tahir, a caravan-factor, was lost on the Hourglass Erg in 412. The term of mourning for a husband is 300 days, and her House posted her "overdue" in 415. His crate lies in the Stasis Vaults, forty steps down, and she visits on Nulldays. Crate contracts run three years and are renewed on Dial Day; she let his run out. "The book of the indecent". Burial in the Erg at dusk, "the dunes holding a fifth". The orchard people of the west mourn with no term. | — |
| lib_f_carrying_of_ketil | The Carrying of Ketil | anonymous, as said at **Skarth** in the Iron Peaks, written down by a carter | c. 409 | poetry (saga) | shelf, Conservatory | Said standing. Ketil keeps goats on the high shelf; his daughter is Hild and his son Arnulf ("light"). The tide-month. The dead are stood up at the standing-ground under **the Tooth**, with stones about the knees. Hild steps back in her own footprints at the dead note. | — |
| lib_f_long_mole_songs | Songs of the Long Mole: Shanties of the Rust Coast, with Their Soundings | Jacomina Roe, printer, of Broadsheet Row | 417 | songbook | pile, below decks on the Kestrel | In 412 a capstan chorus on the Mole brought the first hounds in off the harbour. The coral has gone a hair flat, so pilots sing flat on purpose. Five songs: "What's she singing", which names the **Grinners** (a reef) and the Mole-light and has a new verse, "singing wrong"; "Chorus to You" (the Harbour-Queen dislikes it); "The Mole-Bell"; "The Bottle" (open it at sea, in the dark, ear to the reed, maul to the seacock; "a quiet goes to the light"); and "Wendmere Rings Three" (the town drowned whole by the trench in 413, and its bell rings three strokes and a silence every night). | — |
| lib_f_keep_talking_letters | Keep Talking: Letters from the Wall | Wystan Hobday, penal legionnaire One-Forty-Seven of Null-Watch, to his sister Meggy of Greenroot Hamlet | 415–417 | letters | pile, Greenroot chapel | Kept in the back of the Greenroot parish book. He took the Reeve's mutton in the hungry winter. His partner was Two-Thirty, a Peaks man, who "went to listen" in Frostmonth 415. "The Refusal" is the dawn volley; the General's four flat blows; monks in lead hoods; the unsaid word. A Fenwick man is nicknamed "the Almanac". The Discharge ritual. The letters are read by a clerk at the Organum. He died in Reedmonth 417 when the breech of the second gun on **Battery Eleven** let go. Fifty-Two signs the last letter: "Wystan Hobday, of Greenroot. Discharged." | — |
| lib_f_constancy_of_the_light | On the Constancy of the Light: A Treatise for the Provinces | Canon Placidus Sorell "of the Basilica, Sol-Luminary", translated by Eustace Garner, curate of Oakhaven's Chapel | 410 | philosophy | shelf, Conservatory | The laws are ranked, Light first, and the Sovereign rings at 528. "Casts a shadow at noon". Sol-Luminary is built so that no wall shadows another, and its children fall sick below the cloud. "A greater offering is being prepared, a unison." "A valley where the rain stands still." The Sol-Luminary rose is "the reddest thing in creation". | — |
| lib_f_lantern_on_the_basin | A Lantern on the Basin: A Widow's Journey to Whisper-Grove | Tabitha Fernly, of Greenroot Hamlet | 415 | travel | shelf, Moot-house (World) | Her son Rafe was lost at the Greenroot reed-beds in 412. She heard of the lanterns from a Greenroot carter's wife who is Woods-born on her mother's side. The road east goes through the orchards. Grove speech: "little mother", "dear one", "dearling". She lodged with the **Lantry house** in the Bell Yard: nine people, Isbet (80) and Nonie (6). On Lantern Night 70,000 come; each sets a lantern and says a name, and the people nearest say it after. The Moot-house keeps a shelf of travellers' books. Forty copies were printed at Oakhaven. | — |
| lib_f_bastion_instructions | Instructions to Persons Visiting the Bastion | Keld Holm, Clerk of Visitors, Martial Compact | 416 | travel (regulations) | shop, Odile, 2 | Issued at the **Lower Gate**; fifteen rules. Packs are held by the strap; iron goes sideways in the mist on the outer stair. If the Sounders' tapping stops, go back. New names in the Weighing Hall are cut from ladders. A visitor who asked about standing burial was sent away. Sovereigns are taken, Chimes are not. When the Wall bell is struck in threes, any door opens to a stranger, "the oldest law in Fortis". A visitors' row by the west door records lost visitors' weight. | — |
| lib_f_ruddock_timber_run | Ledger and Day-Book of a Timber Run, Stillwater to Fortis | Rowland Ruddock, timber factor of Stillwater | 416 | travel (ledger) | shelf, Bonded Store | Itemised costs. The raftsmen are Huw, Dickon and Bartle, with his nephew Seb (15). Log ends are wrapped in fleece. At **the Drop** (a weir) a log rang and was given to the hounds. The **Ironford** railhead on the Iron Tramway. The long loop round the Valley of Velocity, where rain has hung since 398. There are no trains on Nullday in the Peaks. "Middling" is an insult. The logs weighed a fifth more at Fortis and sold at 2 sovereigns each. Fortis buys Reach iron-bough for deep-level props, because a ringing prop warns the Sounders three levels off. Seb stays on as a Sounder's apprentice. | — |

#### Whisper-Grove and the Veil

| id | Title | In-world author | Date (SR) | Kind | Where | What it asserts (new facts) | Ruling |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| lib_g_lamp_book | The Lamp Book of the Bell Yard | the Fellowship of Lamp-Makers and Lighters of Whisper-Grove, kept by Osmer Lowry, warden | 396–415 | chronicle | quest (shelver), lying in Whisper-Grove; flyleaf names Osmer and Kitto Lowry go into the Ledger | Six ordinances, including the lost-child rule, no lamp left half-lit, the Moot-door lamp, free lantern paper, and "Goodnight, Grove". There are nine rounds; the Ashlane's is the longest, and the Bell Yard's (eleven lamps) the oldest. Osmer became warden in 396. In 398 cheap Reach rape oil came. Kitto was bound in 401. The water of 407 was the highest in 30 years, and 4,000 papers were given out. In 412 the flame turned paler. In 413 the Fellowship voted 11 to 0 to keep lighting: "A lit street is a street where somebody is expected home". In 415 Kitto was made journeyman at Canopy. | r6, r10 |
| lib_g_midwife_daybook | The Day-Book of a Midwife of the Ashlane | Grisel Imrie, midwife | 414–416 | journal | quest (shelver), the catacombs; flyleaf Grisel Imrie | **Eighth-day naming**: the name is said over a pool by three mouths. She has delivered the cooper's girl, Petronel, Bede, and the twins Columb and Sabra. The willow-cutters. In Lambmonth 416 the lamp-man's boy married. In Sheafmonth 416 Office clerks came wanting every child since 400 "with the date and the house and the voice"; she gave the names but not her book. The works at the end of the Bell Yard grind all night. Her count: 1,091. | **r12**: the hint stays |
| lib_g_grove_primer | The Little Grove Primer | the Moot schools; written in by Blanche and Wilmot Clough of the Carters' Yard | 413 edn, written in until 416 | children's | shelf, Moot-house; flyleaf Blanche and Wilmot Clough | The alphabet (C is the Canopy, which "keeps the whole Grove"). Courtesies: "little mother", and asking a name is not rude. If lost, say your name, your street and your mother's name. The Lesson for Quiet (Moot order, 413) adds a second half to the skipping rhyme: "So sing in the middle, and sing it soft, and never sing in a rush". "It WAS Mam." | r10, r12 |
| lib_g_basin_table | Receipts of the Basin Table | Edith Amory of the Bell Yard, with her daughter Rosamund | 405–415 | cookery | quest (shelver), the Malting; flyleaf Edith and Rosamund Amory | Never cook a fish from a remembering pool. Lantern cakes. The Moot bans fires on the plaza, so broth is carried down in a straw box. Moot pasties. The **remembering loaf** for Canopy is scored once per name and broken, never cut, and a candle is set facing in, "for the house and not for the street". Edith died in Thawmonth 414 ("the Echo will have caught her"). | **r6**: the candle facing in, before 416, stands |
| lib_g_blue_ribbon | Letters in a Blue Ribbon | Cressida Tilney, copy-clerk to the Moot, and Corbet Ansty, journeyman pipe-voicer; bound by her | 414–416 | letters | quest (shelver), the Wick-house; flyleaf Cressida Tilney and Corbet Ansty | The **pipe-voicer** trade in the Organum's east rank: a pipe is cut "until it stops arguing". The Moot sat nine hours on the colonnade paint. Old Ansty of the Cloister road. In the Grove, a thing that matters is said aloud on the water. He proposed by letter in Stillmonth 415 and was due home before Canopy 416. The ribbon is blue, like those on the Moot rolls. | r10 |
| lib_g_plaza_and_canopy | The Plaza and the Canopy, as the Old People Tell It | Carrick Thackray, clerk to the Moot (31 years shelving), for his grandson Joliffe | 411 | history | shelf, Moot-house; flyleaf Carrick and Joliffe Thackray | The Canopy was the first willow, and its roots run under the Woods. "The Echo catches them, and the Canopy keeps them." The Church's man says it is the Spire of Memory. The plaza was laid "before the Reckoning" by masons paid in supper, so Grove masons still take only supper. There are two origin stories for the lanterns. The Moot rules: speak once on a question (twice if very old), and nothing is decided until the door-lamp is lit; the longest sitting ran nine days, over a stair rail. Rolls are tied in blue; the cider barrel has not leaked in 14 years. The town's books are shelved by size, not by name. The House copied the Moot's rolls once a generation. | r10, r12 ("the Canopy keeps the dead") |
| lib_g_bistre_commonplace | The Commonplace Book of the Scribe Styled Bistre | Bistre, scribe of the House | c. 371–414 | journal | shelf, Scriptorium | Designations pass from one holder to the next; the last Bistre left her a cracked horn. The ink receipt: oak-galls from the Organum road, never pool water. Lampblack is soot from the east stove. The Registrar's precept, said in 405. The Memoryday stair-prayer: "I am only the one who comes." Lanterns are set from the steps under the arch. Grey ink is poor for a thousand years. The Prior: "Feel it on the stairs." The minium turned brown in 412. The stacks are "like… a crowded room where everyone is being introduced". | r8 |
| lib_g_pitched_hand_exercises | Exercises in the Pitched Hand | a novice whose name was scraped off, corrected in minium by Woad | 388 | manual | shelf, Scriptorium | The pitched hand writes a word as it is said, with six marks and five heights. The motto has seven sounds. The stove smokes until the third bell. The fourth desk on the south side. | r4, r8 |
| lib_g_orpiment_meditations | Meditations on the Keeping of Names | Orpiment, scribe of the House | 413–Ashfall 416 | philosophy | shelf, Scriptorium | She gave her name 44 years ago. Orpiment is a poison-yellow, ground wet, and fading since 412. She has gone to the stacks eleven times: "I felt expected". "Units". The vellum went in four wagons. She was chosen for the Grove register and goes down in Ashfall 416. "We only kept the door on the latch." | r8, r12 |
| lib_h_daybook | The Duty-Scribe's Day-Book | Ochre, duty-scribe | 416 | journal | found, Scriptorium | Fourteen at the desks, then sixteen. Old Gall's hand is going. The third desk from the east, north side (Sable) finishes the **Fenmark** register and is given the **Mossbank** strays. The Office asked for the House's bell. The Prior says "the Office is the Veil under another name". Gloved Office clerks call the books "Units". The vellum goes down the hill in four wagons. The House is closed for the winter: most scribes go to the Organum and some to the Grove "to take names". | r4, r8 |

### The libraries and their keepers

Sources: `js/libraries26.js`, `js/library26.js`, `tools/library/placement.json`, `tools/i18n/p26-libraries.tsv`, and commits `a581fb4`, `9e77182`, `73b69cf`, `636c236` and `fd20732`. The module header marks as [PROPOSED]: "the Conservatory's place in Oakhaven, Odile Quayle, the Bonded Store, the Moot-house library, the shelver".

| Library | Host map, door, part | Room | Keeper | Shelves (books) | Quest, and what the player gets |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **The Conservatory Reading Room** | Oakhaven, door [48,39] ("The Conservatory"); Parts 1–2 | 13×10, a house wall and wood floor. Intro: *"Reeds hang over the door, cut fresh, whistling under their breath. Inside, books, and the smell of slate-dust."* A hush-lamp; shelf card "READ HERE. TAKE NOTHING. THE REEDS ARE COUNTING."; a **returns shelf** with a card "RETURNS". | **Ilse Brennan, Cantor-Master** (writes on a slate). Topics: *The Conservatory* ("Forty Cantors went out of this room. I kept the books because I could not keep the voices"); *Her voice* ("The Debt of Overtone. Eleven years ago I sang one note too many for a dying boy. He lived. The note kept my voice."); *Lyra* ("My best pupil and my worst listener"); *The Cantors* ("Older than the Synod"). | 12 on shelves: Orchard Craft, First History, The Pruner, Year of Nights, Shepherd's Ear, Fireside Tales, Under a Hand's Width, Tuppence, Ketil, Yewdale Herbal, Off the Slate, Constancy of the Light | **Lost books with the reed stamp**, lent out before the Hedge closed: Ears After Dark (underhedge), Telling the Bees (flume_road), The Lamplighter's Second Note (orla_house), The Wool Wall (p22_hanger). Each return pays 4 gold and 25 XP. **Reward:** she teaches the **tending breath** ("Trees hear breath before voice. So do children."). If the player already knows it, she gives a 30-gold purse instead ("RUDE"). |
| **The Bonded Store** | Stillwater Landing (flume_landing), door [30,8]; Parts 1–2 | 12×10, wood. Intro: *"Tea-chests stencilled TEA and full of paper. Bales of broadsheets under oilcloth. A counter, a lamp, a woman reading."* A lamp shaded on the river side. | **Odile Quayle, book-runner** [PROPOSED]. She sells "a sovereign or two a book". Topics: *Port Cantabile* ("Four papers on the Row…"; "Nobody in the Reach drinks that much tea"); *The lockkeeper* (reads and sells everything); *Bastien Corvo* ("My best customer and my worst debtor… He faints"). | 10 on shelves: Four Seconds, A Good Narrow Cry, Between the Falls, The Still Wheel, Hand on It, The Overdue, Reed and Lamp, The Undertow, The Honest Fire, Ruddock Timber Run. **Sells** Fairweather's Companion (3) and Bastion Instructions (2). | **A wet crate** of books lost in the Low Rings "when the surge came early": Saffery Lightfoot and Going Up. Each pays 12 gold and 25 XP. **Reward:** she writes your name in her book; "Friends of the Store" pay ×0.75 ("Do not tell Corvo"). |
| **The Moot-house** | Whisper-Grove, door [40,27]; Part 3 | 15×11, grey marble, dusty shelves, benches in rows, dry lamps. Intro: *"The town met here. The benches are still in rows. Along the walls, the town's books, shelved by size, not by name."* Shelf text: "Some of the flyleaves have names on them. Some have had them cut out." | **A Hollow among the shelves** ("…by size… not by name…", "…somebody will want it…"). He is unnamed (r10) and is not identified as Carrick Thackray. | 3 on shelves: Lantern on the Basin, Plaza and Canopy, Grove Primer | The quest opens when the player reads a book with **names on its flyleaf**; the names go into the Ledger. The four named books: Lamp Book (whispergrove), Midwife's Day-Book (catacombs), Basin Table (malting), Blue Ribbon (wickhouse). **Reward:** a book "kept for someone who did not come": *The Eight Keepers*. |
| **The Scriptorium of the Silent Veil** | the Mausoleum (House of the Silent Veil), door [1,20], "A stair up to the scriptorium"; Part 4 | 17×8. *"The first floor, the east end, where the light comes in first. Two rows of sloped desks. A stove at each end, cold."* There are 8 desks in the build. One desk is labelled "The third desk from the east end, on the north side. Someone sat here for years." There is a west stove and an east stove ("The duty-scribe lit this one first"). Shelf text: "The Office took the vellum to the Organum. What is left is what it did not want: the scribes' own books, shelved in the gaps." | none | 5 on shelves: What Is Written, The Rule of the House, Bistre's Commonplace, Exercises in the Pitched Hand, Orpiment's Meditations. **Found:** the Duty-Scribe's Day-Book. | — |

**Elsewhere** (`placement.json`):
- **16 piles:**
  - the Greenhall: Greenroot Road and the Prospectus;
  - the Chapel: Fires on the Ground and the Only Song;
  - the watch-house: the Digest;
  - the surgery: the Grafting Handbook;
  - the Bellwether: Rudd's book and the Bellwether Book;
  - Greenroot chapel: the Little Catechism and Keep Talking;
  - the clearing: the Fellers' Book and the Reeds-Miner;
  - Depot Three: Letters from the Gatehouse;
  - the Deep Woods: Already Quiet;
  - below decks on the Kestrel: Tank and Envelope and Long Mole Songs.
- **Vendors:**
  - Bastien Corvo, 1 each: the Almanack, the Last Sheaf play and the Narrow Larder;
  - Vane, 3: *A True and Modest Account*;
  - Odile: as above.
- **Found in the world:** Stone, Then Glass (the quarry).
- **Total:** 30 shelved + 16 piles + 6 sold + 10 quest + 2 found + 1 gift = **65**.

**Pile texts that read as canon:**
- the tavern: "Hetty lends them on trust";
- the Malting: "Books the Office did not catalogue, or did and forgot";
- Lyra's house: "kept… away from the reeds";
- the clearing: a crate under the hut eaves;
- the wharf: "a tea-chest… for sale or swap, no questions".

**How books work** (`library26.js`):
- The six shelves are Histories and chronicles; Lives, letters and journals; Tales and verse; Craft and the natural world; Faith, law and the press; and The wider world.
- Reading never consumes a book and earns +1 Lore and 10 XP.
- Milestones: "A Reader of the Reach" (10 read), "Keeper of Shelves" (25), "Fifty Spines" (50), and "The Library of the Reach" (all).
- The logbook's "Your Library" counts what is not yet found in each region.

**The serial's placements** (`js/books.js`):
- **Bastien Corvo, pedlar of Cantabile broadsheets**, sits at the Boar & Dulcimer in the wharf's river coat. He sells at 1 sovereign a part. On Broadsheet Row he says: "Four papers on the Row… This is none of the four… The lockkeeper there reads everything I carry, and sells what he reads."
- **Hetty's donated bundle** lies by the east table, "paid for by a carter", with the note "PAID FOR. TAKE ONE OF EACH. LEAVE THE CANTOR HER COPIES."
- **The Cantor's lending shelf** in Lyra's house is removed once `rite_started` is set.

### Canon the books add to the world

Format: **fact**, then status, then source book, then Bible location.

#### People of the Reach

- **Orrin Pale was Oakhaven's sexton already in 412 and wrote a chronicle of the hard winter.** **canon (ruled)** (r13). Source: lib_a_hard_winter. Bible: new; game text has him as the Chapel sexton (Phase 16; d73de90).
- **The hard winter's named dead:** Wilf and Alban Wetherby, Essa Brindle, Tilda Hale (9), Mother Peascod, Lettie, Osgood and the baby (the miller Lambert Coker's), Jonas Garlick, Hobday's infant, the Sawtell brothers, Widow Dimmock, old Hesketh's sister, two Ackroyds and an unnamed Greenroot carter. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_a_hard_winter. Bible: new (MS ch.2 has only "the Wetherby twins, the Hale girl, the miller's three and eleven more"); Essa at the sluice is in game text, js/tobin.js p.3.
- **Dorcas Ribble, a carder at the Carders' mill, proposed the Hedge.** **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_a_wool_wall. Bible: new (Tobin's journal p.10 has "a carder from the mill").
- **Josiah Seddon** has been under-clerk at the Greenhall for 11 years and keeps copies in his press. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_a_greenroot_road. Bible: new.
- **Cuthbert Tench** is an almanack-maker printing at Stillwater Landing. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_a_almanack_419. Bible: new.
- **Ezra Dunnock** is a farmer of the north field, Oakhaven, with a daughter of 8; the last stand of 419 is his. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_a_almanack_419. Bible: new.
- **Jessamy Yarrow** was a Cantor of Reedside in 396, taught by **Mother Sefton**. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_a_orchard_craft. Bible: new.
- **Garra Thorne wrote "A First History of Oakhaven" for the orchard school (spring 419).** **canon (ruled)** (r13). Source: lib_a_first_history. Bible: Garra Thorne is already in Vol V §Other Reach Figures; the book is new.
- **Winifred "Win" Ruddock** (c. 317–391) was the Cantor of the longest reed-row; her pupil was **Jennet Lindley** of the dye-works. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_b_the_pruner. Bible: new.
- **Silas Hurlock**, quarryman, married to **Sukey**, trained under **Master Cleeve**. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_b_stone_then_glass. Bible: new.
- **The bore-head deaths: Marten, Old Coyle and the Tanner twins.** **canon (book)**. Source: lib_b_stone_then_glass. Bible: Series Bible §Audit — Close of Part One (Tanner twins); MS ch.5 l.1747.
- **Cyril Tolley**, junior Consortium clerk at the works gate, and his sister **Prudence Tolley**. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_b_letters_from_the_gate. Bible: new.
- **Godwin Pettigrew**, lamplighter (212 lamps, tannery steps to the top of Cooper's Row); **Tibby Lockwood**, night-hummer at Well Corner; **Abigail Starling** (70), taken from the end house by the dye-works. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_b_year_of_nights. Bible: new.
- **Tomas was a "reeds-miner" before 412. He was 33 then, has a wife, and his grey mare Dumpling bites.** **canon (ruled)** (r13). Source: lib_b_reeds_miner. Bible: Tomas is already in Vol II §People You Can Use Today; the facts are new.
- **Tomas lost three fingers to the saw in 418; the fourth grew back wrong.** **canon (book)**. Source: lib_b_reeds_miner and lib_d_fellers_book. Bible: Vol II §People; the year 418 is **[PROPOSED]**.
- **Gwen Fitch** is Tomas's sister's daughter. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_b_reeds_miner. Bible: new.
- **Jethro Nettleford** was Oakhaven's feller-master (died c. 412); Tomas was his 12th and last apprentice; **Jago Holcombe** lost an arm to a tree felled awake. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_d_fellers_book. Bible: new.
- **Owen Hatherly** of Wethercombe, a scholar at the Conservatory of Bores from 414 who left in 416; **Master Hollister**; **Brann** of the Peaks; **Tarquin** of the Organum (vanished). **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_b_shepherds_ear. Bible: new.
- **Margery Fallow** keeps the little school on Cooper's Row. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_c_fireside_tales. Bible: new.
- **Elspeth Garland**, author of a children's picture-book. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_c_tuppence_narrow. Bible: new.
- **Mercy Hatchard** (24) and **Gabe Hatchard**, coopers of Cooper's Row; **Crispin Whitlock**, a stammering lamplighter; **Mr Osgood**, a works clerk; neighbours **the Bramleys** and **the widow Ferrier**. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_c_lamplighters_note. Bible: new.
- **Edwy Marchbank**, reed-pruner for 31 years, now sweeps the Chapel; his wife is **Hannah**. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_c_under_a_hands_width. Bible: new.
- **Reuben Thrale** kept the Bellwether until Reed-Hanging 416, when **Gideon Thrale**, his nephew, took over. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_c_bellwether_book. Bible: new; game text Phase 22 says Gideon "had it from my uncle".
- **Cicely** "from the fold" made the Low Song (winter 414); **A. L.** was a drover. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_c_bellwether_book. Bible: new.
- **Ursula Dimmock** set down the Last Sheaf play; **Hodge Lorkin** answered the Voice in 418. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_c_last_sheaf_play. Bible: new.
- **Barnabas Yewdale**, physician of Greenroot, author of a herbal (391); **Prue Gorse**, herb-woman late of Greenroot, now inside the Hedge; **Jem Cutler**. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_d_yewdale_herbal. Bible: new.
- **Tilda Dunnock** has been night-hummer at the Tannery Row crossing since the Hedge closed. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_d_ears_after_dark. Bible: new.
- **Philippa Oatley** kept the cookshop by the market. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_d_narrow_larder. Bible: new.

- **Dorran Sedge wrote "Reed and Lamp"; he tended the Reedside reed-beds before the river gate; his wife is Mabbie.** **canon (ruled)** (r13). Source: lib_d_reed_and_lamp. Bible: Vol V §Other Reach Figures and §The Grove of Oakhaven.
- **Absalom Rudd wrote "Rudd's Back-End Book" and sits at Gideon's window.** **canon (ruled)** (r13). Source: lib_d_rudds_almanac. Bible: new (game text Phase 22).

- **The Farrants** of the sheep-walk drove the flocks to the Hedge; a Farrant girl fell in the Ram's trench in 409. **[PROPOSED]**, though the Farrants themselves are Phase 22 text. Source: lib_a_wool_wall, lib_c_bellwether_book. Bible: new.
- **Ilse Brennan lost her voice in the throat-fever of 408, after nine nights, and teaches by slate.** **canon (book)**. Source: lib_e_off_the_slate. Bible: Vol V §Ilse Brennan (78).
- **Hazel Thrup**, Ilse's pupil, copied her sayings. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_e_off_the_slate. Bible: new.

#### People of Whisper-Grove and the Veil

All the Grove names are before 416; the practice is allowed by r10 and the names are carried to the Ledger from the flyleaves.

- **Osmer Lowry**, warden of the Lamp-Makers and Lighters (from 396), and his sister's son **Kitto Lowry** (bound 401, journeyman 415, married 416). **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_g_lamp_book, lib_g_midwife_daybook. Bible: new.
- **Grisel Imrie**, midwife of the Ashlane (1,091 births), behind the blue door at the top of the steps. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_g_midwife_daybook. Bible: new.
- **Infants named on the water:** Petronel, Bede, and the fuller's twins Columb and Sabra (415). **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_g_midwife_daybook. Bible: new.
- **Blanche and Wilmot Clough** of the Carters' Yard. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_g_grove_primer. Bible: new.
- **Edith Amory** of the Bell Yard (died Thawmonth 414) and her daughter **Rosamund**. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_g_basin_table. Bible: new.
- **Cressida Tilney**, copy-clerk to the Moot; **Corbet Ansty**, journeyman pipe-voicer; **Old Ansty of the Cloister road**. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_g_blue_ribbon. Bible: new.
- **Carrick Thackray**, clerk to the Moot, who shelved the town's books for 31 years, and his grandson **Joliffe**. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_g_plaza_and_canopy. Bible: new.
- **The Lantry house** of the Bell Yard: nine people, with old **Isbet** (80) and **Nonie** (6). **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_f_lantern_on_the_basin. Bible: new.
- **Veil scribes are styled by ink and pigment names that pass from holder to holder: Umber, Bistre, Woad, Orpiment, Ochre and Gall.** **canon (ruled)** (r8: "stand [PROPOSED]"). Source: the g_ and h_ books. Bible: new; Sable as "a Veil designation" is in Series Bible §Part I.
- **Umber** held the designation 51 years by 410 and volunteered for the Grove register; an earlier Umber copied the Rule c. 290. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_a_what_is_written, lib_e_veil_rule. Bible: new. *See Contradictions: the game's 419 Umber NPC.*
- **The Prior of the House** writes and seals scribes' names. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_e_veil_rule, lib_g_bistre_commonplace, lib_h_daybook. Bible: new (the bible's "Prior" hits are the Cloister's Sub-Prior and a Rust-Coast Prior's receiving-book in Vol V).
- **Sable's desk: the third desk from the east, north side.** **canon (book)**. Source: lib_h_daybook, lib_g_orpiment_meditations, the scriptorium room. Bible: Vol V §The House of the Silent Veil. (The MS has "by the second window".)
- **The Registrar said "Rule the page before you write on it…" to an apprentice in 405.** **canon (book)** (MS ch.22 epigraph). Source: lib_g_bistre_commonplace. Bible: Pellam Quist is in Series Bible §The Iron Synod.

- **The Chalk Ram, scoured in 395, 402 and 409; not scoured in 416.** **canon (book)** for the Ram (Phase 22), with the dates **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_c_bellwether_book. Bible: new.

- **The Drop**, a weir below Stillwater, and **Ironford**, railhead of the Iron Tramway. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_f_ruddock_timber_run. Bible: new; the Iron Tramway is in Series Bible §The Tempest Archipelago.
- **The Conservatory has a library and a Reading Room; the calling-song is practised on its roof.** **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_a_orchard_craft, libraries26. Bible: Reedside Conservatory is in Vol II §Settlements and Vol V §Ilse Brennan.
- **Port Cantabile:** the mast-tower is 60 ft of iron rungs with a cradle at the end of the Long Mole, roped to the Mole-head derrick. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_a_going_up. Bible: the Long Mole and derrick are in Vol IV §The town as written; the tower is new.
- **The drift-yard below the Long Mole.** **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_d_tank_and_envelope. Bible: new; the drift-bench is in MS l.6328.
- **The Grinners**, a reef off Port Cantabile. **[PROPOSED]**. Source: lib_f_long_mole_songs. Bible: new.
- **Wendmere was a fishing town south of the Cay, drowned whole when the trench opened under it in 413; its bell rings three strokes and a silence nightly.** **canon (book)**, with the year **[PROPOSED]** but matching Vol V's "Synod bored the trench in '13". Source: lib_f_long_mole_songs. Bible: Vol IV §Wendmere and the Chime; Vol V §Status.
- **The Endless**, a wind south of Cape Dissonance blowing west since 412. **[PROPOSED]**, and only inside a penny tale. Source: lib_c_saffery_lightfoot. Bible: new.
